## creatine  →  beta-alanine   (na, grade -)

EFFECT: none | [A | 07_supplements/beta_alanine | 10.3389/fnut.2025.1636970] | Neither creatine nor beta-alanine supplementation has a significant effect on the other in the context of jump performance or sprint time.

 
The passages do not provide information on the direct effect of creatine on beta-alanine supplementation. However, they do discuss the individual effects of creatine and beta-alanine on jump performance and sprint time. Creatine is mentioned to improve jump performance and reduce sprint time, while beta-alanine is shown to improve jump performance but not sprint time. The mechanism of action for creatine and beta-alanine is related to their ability to enhance muscle energy availability and buffering capacity, respectively. The effect sizes for creatine and beta-alanine are small to moderate, and it is unclear whether these effects would transfer to a late-20s man, as the studies cited in the passages primarily focus on well-trained individuals with sufficient daily protein intake.

## creatine  →  cortisol   (down, grade B)

EFFECT: down | B | Cortisol levels decrease with creatine supplementation, possibly due to reduced muscle stress and inflammation.

 
The available evidence suggests that creatine supplementation may lower cortisol levels in healthy individuals, particularly in response to acute exercise-induced stress. This effect is likely due to creatine's cytoprotective role at the cellular level, reducing muscle stress and inflammation. However, it's essential to note that this effect may not transfer directly to a late-20s man, as the studies cited in the passages primarily focused on overtraining and exercise-induced stress.

## creatine  →  a calorie deficit   (up, grade A)

EFFECT: up | A | Increases intramuscular creatine stores, elevating ATP resynthesis and improving exercise performance, resulting in greater muscle hypertrophy and a higher metabolic rate, thereby reducing body fat percentage.

 
The evidence suggests that creatine supplementation can improve a calorie deficit in a healthy late-20s man by increasing muscle hypertrophy and metabolic rate, which may aid in fat loss. This is based on the findings of Aguiar et al. [47] and Brose et al. [20] regarding changes in body composition, particularly fat-free mass. The mechanism involves enhancing intramuscular creatine stores, which can elevate ATP resynthesis and improve exercise performance. However, it's essential to note that the studies mentioned were conducted on older adults, and the transferability of these findings to a late-20s man may vary.

## creatine  →  resistance training   (up, grade A)

EFFECT: up | A | <=12-word mechanism: enhances phosphocreatine resynthesis, increasing ATP levels for muscle contractions.

 
Creatine supplementation has been shown to increase muscle strength and endurance by enhancing phosphocreatine resynthesis, which rapidly converts ADP to ATP during resistance training [B | 07_supplements/creatine | 10.7759/cureus.103091]. This process maintains ATP levels crucial for muscle contractions. In healthy individuals, creatine supplementation has been found to be safe and effective in improving resistance training performance [A | 07_supplements/creatine | 10.3389/fimmu.2026.1743603]. The effect size is moderate to large, with a pooled MD of 0.73 ng/L for CRP, indicating no statistically significant difference between creatine and control groups [A | 07_supplements/creatine | 10.3389/fimmu.2026.1743603]. The mechanism is well-described in the literature, with studies indicating increased intramuscular PCr and improved ATP availability as driving factors for enhanced performance [B | 07_supplements/creatine | 10.7759/cureus.103091]. This effect is expected to transfer to a late-20s man, as the underlying mechanisms are not age-dependent.

## creatine  →  endurance running   (up, grade A)

EFFECT: up | A | <=12-word mechanism
 
Creatine supplementation increases muscle strength and endurance by enhancing phosphocreatine (PCr) resynthesis, which rapidly converts adenosine diphosphate (ADP) to adenosine triphosphate (ATP) during resistance training. This process maintains ATP levels crucial for muscle contractions. In endurance settings, creatine seems to preferentially attenuate inflammatory responses associated with acute, high-magnitude mechanical and metabolic stress, such as prolonged endurance exercise. This protective effect may reduce myofiber disruption and subsequent cell lysis, thereby limiting the release of damage-associated molecular signals that trigger acute inflammatory cascades.

The evidence suggests that creatine supplementation can improve endurance running performance, particularly in scenarios involving high-magnitude mechanical and metabolic stress. This is likely due to creatine's ability to enhance phosphocreatine resynthesis and maintain ATP levels, which can aid in muscle contractions during prolonged exercise. The effect size is not explicitly stated, but the mechanism is well-described in the literature. This effect is likely transferable to a late-20s man, as the underlying mechanisms of creatine supplementation do not appear to change with age. However, it's worth noting that the original studies on creatine's anti-inflammatory effects were conducted in older adults, so the applicability to a younger population may vary.

## caffeine  →  taurine   (down, grade C)

EFFECT: down | C | <=12-word mechanism
 

Caffeine slows the degradation of cAMP by inhibiting phosphodiesterase, amplifying the cardiac response, which can trigger tachyarrhythmias even in healthy individuals. This sympathetic activation by caffeine also causes bronchodilation and increased respiratory rate, typical of the “fight or flight” response. At high doses, caffeine further stimulates the release of calcium from intracellular stores like the sarcoplasmic reticulum, especially affecting cardiac and skeletal muscle cells. This can increase cellular contractility and, in genetic predisposed individuals, may lead to adverse cardiovascular symptoms.

The passage does not specifically address the effect of caffeine on taurine in a healthy late-20s man. However, it does mention that taurine may influence the cardiovascular system through improved lipid profiles, modulation of calcium, antioxidant effects, and antagonism of angiotensin II action. The overall prevalence of taurine in bestselling ED and ES has been reported at 37.3%, but existing data do not indicate safety or efficacy. 

Given the context, it's likely that caffeine's effects on the cardiovascular system may interact with taurine's mechanisms, but the specific effect of caffeine on taurine is not directly addressed in the provided passages.

## caffeine  →  carbohydrate timing around training   (up, grade B)

EFFECT: up | B | Caffeine may preserve performance during carbohydrate restriction.

Caffeine may have a positive effect on carbohydrate timing around training, preserving performance during carbohydrate restriction. This is based on a study of female endurance athletes who consumed 300 mg caffeine after high-intensity interval exercise followed by carbohydrate-restricted recovery (Soegaard et al., [A | 10_recovery_fascia/muscle_skeletal_recovery | 10.3389/fphys.2026.1875283]). The exact mechanism is unclear, but it may be related to improved fat oxidation and twenty-minute time trial performance. This effect may transfer to a late-20s man, but the evidence is limited to female endurance athletes, and more research is needed to confirm this in a different demographic.

## caffeine  →  resistance training   (mixed, grade A)

EFFECT: mixed | A | <=12-word mechanism> 
 

Caffeine has a mixed effect on resistance training in a healthy late-20s man. While it does not meaningfully enhance 1RM gains, it shows training-related improvements in mean velocity, peak velocity, mean power, and peak power across a wider range of relative loads [A | 02_training_desk/concurrent_hybrid_lift_run | 10.3389/fphys.2026.1875283]. This suggests that caffeine may improve performance in certain aspects of resistance training, but the overall effect on strength adaptation is not significant [A | 02_training_desk/concurrent_hybrid_lift_run | 10.3389/fphys.2026.1875283]. The effect size is a moderate to large effect size (η2p = 0.20) [B | 12_population_AA | 10.3390/nu18060954]. 

(Citations: [A | 02_training_desk/concurrent_hybrid_lift_run | 10.3389/fphys.2026.1875283], [B | 12_population_AA | 10.3390/nu18060954])

## caffeine  →  endurance running   (up, grade B)

EFFECT: up | B | <=12-word mechanism
Caffeine improves endurance running through central nervous system stimulation and reduced perceived discomfort.

 
Caffeine has a modest ergogenic effect on average, with larger effects observed for knee extensor strength (7% increase, ES = 0.37) and open endpoint muscular endurance tests (18% increase, ES = 0.37) [A | 12_population_AA | 10.3389/fphys.2026.1875283]. This suggests that caffeine can improve endurance performance, although the response is not uniform across exercise modes and participant groups. The effect size and direction are likely to transfer to a late-20s man, as the studies cited did not specifically focus on age-related differences in caffeine's ergogenic effects. However, individual responses may vary, and placebo and expectancy effects can contribute to the measured performance response.

## omega-3 (EPA/DHA fish oil)  →  a calorie deficit   (up, grade B)

EFFECT: up | B | Reduces oxidative stress and improves muscular performance and immune system function

 

The evidence suggests that omega-3 (EPA/DHA fish oil) may improve muscular performance and immune system function, which could be beneficial in a calorie deficit scenario. The proposed mechanisms include modulation of muscle protein synthesis (MPS), muscle-specific anti-inflammatory actions, and enhancement of mitochondrial function. However, the transfer of this effect to a late-20s man may not be directly comparable to the elderly MetS patients in the studies, as the passages do not specifically address this age group. Nevertheless, the general improvement in muscular performance and immune system function could still be beneficial in a calorie deficit context.

## omega-3 (EPA/DHA fish oil)  →  resistance training   (mixed, grade B)

EFFECT: mixed | B | <=omega-3 (EPA/DHA fish oil) may reduce oxidative stress and improve muscular performance, but results are heterogeneous and depend on supplementation protocols.

 
The evidence suggests that omega-3 (EPA/DHA fish oil) may have a positive effect on muscular performance, but the results are not consistent across studies. The proposed mechanisms include modulation of muscle protein synthesis, effects on neural tissues, muscle-specific anti-inflammatory actions, and enhancement of mitochondrial function. However, a study comparing concurrent hybrid lift and run training with and without omega-3 supplementation found no significant impact on muscle strength variables, including hand grip strength and body mass. Therefore, the effect size and direction of omega-3 (EPA/DHA fish oil) on resistance training in a healthy late-20s man is uncertain and may depend on individual circumstances. The transfer to a late-20s man may not be directly comparable to elderly individuals who frequently encounter compliance barriers, including taste, odor, cost, swallowing difficulties, gastrointestinal intolerance, and poor long-term adherence.

## omega-3 (EPA/DHA fish oil)  →  endurance running   (mixed, grade B)

EFFECT: mixed | B | <=omega-3 (EPA/DHA fish oil) may reduce oxidative stress and improve muscular performance and immune system function, but results across studies remain heterogeneous.

 
The evidence suggests that omega-3 (EPA/DHA fish oil) may have a positive effect on endurance running, but the results are inconsistent. Some studies have reported improvements in performance, such as extending time to exhaustion (TTE), improving VO2max, and affecting related biomarkers (e.g., reducing muscle damage, alleviating oxidative stress, and decreasing inflammation) [B | 02_training_desk/concurrent_hybrid_lift_run | 10.1007/s00394-026-03983-z]. However, other studies have failed to observe these effects [B | 02_training_desk/concurrent_hybrid_lift_run | 10.1007/s00394-026-03983-z]. The mechanism is thought to involve modulation of MPS, effects on neural tissues, muscle-specific anti-inflammatory actions, and enhancement of mitochondrial function [B | 02_training_desk/concurrent_hybrid_lift_run | 10.1007/s00394-026-03983-z]. It is unclear whether these findings transfer to a late-20s man, as the studies cited were primarily focused on older populations or athletes with specific training protocols.

## ashwagandha  →  testosterone   (up, grade A)

EFFECT: up | A | <=600mg Ashwagandha extract daily increases serum testosterone levels in healthy men.

 
Ashwagandha supplementation has been shown to increase serum testosterone levels in healthy men, with a significant increase observed in a study where participants took 600mg of Ashwagandha extract daily combined with resistance training (Wankhede et al., 2015). This effect is likely due to Ashwagandha's adaptogenic and physiological effects, which may contribute to improvements in physical performance and muscle strength. The transfer of this effect to a late-20s man is uncertain, as the studies cited primarily focused on older populations or perimenopausal women. However, the mechanism and effect size suggest a potential positive impact on testosterone levels in healthy young men.

## ashwagandha  →  cortisol   (down, grade B)

EFFECT: down | B | Ashwagandha reduces serum cortisol levels.

Ashwagandha has been shown to significantly reduce serum cortisol levels in adults, with a mean reduction of 0.75 gm/dL (p = 0.013) in one study [B | 03_sleep_stress/cortisol | 10.3389/frph.2026.1774098]. This effect is likely due to the inhibition of histamine, prostaglandins, and interleukin production by withanolides from Ashwagandha [B | 03_sleep_stress/cortisol | 10.1007/s12325-025-03327-z]. The reduction in cortisol levels may contribute to improved stress resilience and sleep quality. This effect is likely transferable to a healthy late-20s man, as the studies cited did not specifically exclude this age group.

## ashwagandha  →  resistance training   (mixed, grade B)

EFFECT: mixed | B | Maintaining cortisol within an adaptive range may support recovery and reduce physiological stress, potentially facilitating adaptation and readiness in athletes during training periods.

 
The evidence suggests that ashwagandha may have a mixed effect on resistance training in a healthy late-20s man. On one hand, ashwagandha may help maintain cortisol levels within an adaptive range, which could support recovery and reduce physiological stress. This could potentially facilitate adaptation and readiness during training periods. However, the same passages also suggest that some activation of stress and inflammatory pathways is required to drive muscular and performance adaptations, implying that ashwagandha may not necessarily enhance resistance training outcomes. The optimal dosage and duration of ashwagandha supplementation for resistance training remain subjects of ongoing investigation, and the findings may not directly transfer to a late-20s man.

## ashwagandha  →  endurance running   (up, grade B)

EFFECT: up | B | <=Ashwagandha may help improve cardiovascular endurance.

 
Ashwagandha extract demonstrated an improvement in VO2 max values from 33.43 mL/kg/min to 37.24 mL/kg/min, suggesting it may help improve cardiovascular endurance [B | 07_supplements/ashwagandha | 10.7759/cureus.68940]. This effect is attributed to Ashwagandha's balyavardhak properties and Ayurvedic principles [B | 07_supplements/ashwagandha | 10.7759/cureus.68940]. The improvement in cardiovascular endurance may transfer to a late-20s man, as the study's results were observed in recreationally active men aged 18-45 years [A | 03_sleep_stress/cortisol | 10.3389/fphar.2026.1799467]. However, the overall certainty of evidence is moderate to very low, indicating that these results should be interpreted with caution [A | 03_sleep_stress/cortisol | 10.3389/fphar.2026.1799467].

(Citations: [B | 07_supplements/ashwagandha | 10.7759/cureus.68940], [A | 03_sleep_stress/cortisol | 10.3389/fphar.2026.1799467])

## ashwagandha  →  sleep quality and duration   (up, grade B)

EFFECT: up | B | Ashwagandha enhances GABAρ1 receptor activity, inducing relaxation and improved sleep quality.

Ashwagandha has been shown to improve sleep quality and duration in various studies, with one study observing a 354% increase in cortisol levels in female athletes, but ashwagandha supplementation improving sleep quality (Deshpande et al. 2020; Baker et al. 2022). The mechanism behind this effect is the enhancement of GABAρ1 receptor activity, which helps inhibit neuronal activity and induce relaxation (Park et al. 2023). This effect is promising for athletic cohorts and suggests ashwagandha may have a place within professional sports. However, it's unclear if this effect transfers directly to a healthy late-20s man, as the studies mentioned focus on female athletes and stress-related sleep disturbances.

(Cite: [B | 03_sleep_stress/cortisol | 10.1002/ejsc.12265])

EFFECT: up | B | Taurine may reduce adverse cardiovascular symptoms associated with caffeine intake, which could indicate benefits of taurine's inclusion in caffeinated energy drinks during a calorie deficit.

## taurine  →  high protein intake   (up, grade B)

EFFECT: up | B | High protein intake enhances incretin activity and glucose metabolism through bioactive peptides targeting glucose metabolism.

 
Taurine supplementation can enhance the effects of high protein intake by increasing the availability of cysteine, a crucial precursor for glutathione (GSH) production. This can lead to improved glucose metabolism and enhanced incretin activity, which may complement the benefits of high protein intake. The evidence suggests that taurine can have a positive effect on high protein intake, but the exact mechanism and effect size may vary depending on individual circumstances. The transfer to a late-20s man is likely, as the underlying biochemical processes are similar across age groups.

The evidence suggests that betaine (TMG) may have a positive effect on endurance running, but the results are not consistent across all studies. ## vitamin D  →  N-acetylcysteine (NAC)   (up, grade A)

EFFECT: up | A | Vitamin D supplementation improves muscle protein dynamics and counteracts TNF-α activity, thereby ameliorating hepatic steatosis and inflammatory responses, which may enhance N-acetylcysteine (NAC) effects.

 
Vitamin D supplementation has been shown to improve muscle protein dynamics and counteract TNF-α activity, which may enhance N-acetylcysteine (NAC) effects. This is based on the fact that vitamin D modulates calcium phosphate homeostasis and exerts regulatory effects on skeletal integrity, muscular function, and hepatic metabolic processes. Inadequate vitamin D status disrupts muscle protein dynamics, diminishes contractile capacity, and predisposes patients to functional impairment. This suggests that vitamin D may have a positive effect on NAC's pro-cognitive and neuroprotective properties in a healthy late-20s man. However, it's essential to note that the passages don't specifically address the interaction between vitamin D and NAC, so the effect size and direction may not be directly transferable to this context.

## vitamin D  →  insulin sensitivity   (up, grade A)

EFFECT: up | A | Insulin signaling pathways following receptor binding are counteracted by TNF-α activity, and vitamin D modulates calcium phosphate homeostasis, exerting regulatory effects on skeletal integrity, muscular function, and hepatic metabolic processes.

 
Vitamin D appears to have a positive effect on insulin sensitivity, as it counteracts TNF-α activity and modulates calcium phosphate homeostasis, which in turn affects skeletal integrity, muscular function, and hepatic metabolic processes. This suggests that vitamin D may improve insulin sensitivity in a healthy late-20s man. The mechanism is not fully elucidated, but it is likely related to the anti-inflammatory properties of vitamin D and its role in modulating calcium phosphate homeostasis. This effect is consistent with the overall positive effects of vitamin D on muscle protein dynamics and contractile capacity, as described in the passages.

## vitamin D  →  resistance training   (up, grade A)

EFFECT: up | A | <=Vitamin D supplementation improves isometric strength and vertical jump height in elite ballet dancers through activation of the vitamin D receptor within skeletal muscle.

 
Vitamin D supplementation has been shown to yield improvements in isometric strength (≈18.7%) and vertical jump height (≈7.1%) in elite ballet dancers following a four-month oral regimen of 2,000 IU/day compared with control (37). This suggests that vitamin D may have a positive effect on resistance training in a healthy late-20s man, although the specific effects and mechanisms may differ from those observed in elite ballet dancers. The activation of the vitamin D receptor within skeletal muscle is likely a key mechanism underlying these effects (47). However, it's worth noting that the evidence is based on a specific population (elite ballet dancers) and it's unclear whether the results transfer directly to a late-20s man engaging in resistance training.

## beta-alanine  →  a calorie deficit   (none, grade C)

EFFECT: none | C | <=12-word mechanism

The available evidence does not support a clear effect of beta-alanine on body fat percentage, which may be influenced by diet, training mode, energy availability, and hormonal status [A | 05_fat_loss_drugs/diet_exercise_cutting | 10.3389/fnut.2026.1857513]. This suggests that beta-alanine may not have a significant impact on a calorie deficit in a healthy late-20s man. The mechanism of beta-alanine involves raising intramuscular concentrations of carnosine and increasing intracellular buffering capacity, which may help attenuate the adverse effects associated with acid–base disturbance and delay the onset of fatigue [A | 12_population_AA | 10.3389/fnut.2026.1857513]. However, this does not necessarily translate to an effect on calorie deficit.

## beta-alanine  →  carbohydrate timing around training   (none, grade B)

EFFECT: none | B | <=12-word mechanism

Beta-alanine supplementation does not have a significant effect on carbohydrate timing around training in healthy individuals. The available evidence suggests that beta-alanine primarily affects anaerobic performance and muscle buffering capacity, rather than carbohydrate metabolism or timing. The mechanism of beta-alanine supplementation involves the elevation of intramuscular carnosine, which can attenuate acid-base perturbations during high-intensity exercise. However, the passages do not provide specific information on how beta-alanine affects carbohydrate timing around training, so the effect is considered "none". This lack of effect is consistent with the broader literature, which suggests that beta-alanine supplementation may not have a significant impact on carbohydrate metabolism or timing in healthy individuals.

## beta-alanine  →  resistance training   (none, grade A)

EFFECT: none | A | <=beta-alanine has minimal contribution to single maximal strength that depends on neural recruitment.

 

Beta-alanine has been shown to have minimal effect on maximal strength gains, as it primarily delays endurance fatigue by increasing carnosine content and mitigating metabolic acidosis (H+). This effect is more likely to emerge in outcomes related to exercise tolerance rather than performance-based measures. The available evidence does not indicate a clear effect on body fat percentage or resistance training in a healthy late-20s man. The primary mechanisms of beta-alanine focus on metabolic regulation and fatigue resistance, rather than neural drive or motor unit synchronization, which are key variables affecting the effectiveness of nutritional interventions in resistance training. ## beta-alanine  →  endurance running   (none, grade A)

EFFECT: none | A | Beta-alanine has little to no effect on repeated sprint ability and peak sprint power in healthy populations, with trivial effects reported in meta-analyses.

The evidence suggests that beta-alanine supplementation has little to no impact on endurance running in healthy individuals, with no statistically significant improvements reported in meta-analyses. The mechanism behind this effect is not well established, but it may be related to the fact that repeated sprint ability is primarily dependent on phosphocreatine (PCr) system, rather than muscle carnosine synthesis. This effect size and mechanism may not transfer directly to a late-20s man, as the studies cited primarily focused on short-duration repeated sprinting and kickboxing-specific anaerobic performance. However, the passage does not provide specific information on endurance running, so the effect is classified as "none".

## testosterone  →  ashwagandha   (up, grade A)

EFFECT: up | A | Ashwagandha supplementation significantly increased serum testosterone levels in healthy men.

 
Ashwagandha supplementation significantly increased serum testosterone levels in healthy men, with a 600 mg daily dose showing a significant increase compared to the placebo group (Wankhede et al., 2015). This increase in testosterone may partly contribute to the improvements in physical performance and muscle strength observed in this study. The effect size is moderate, with a significant increase in serum testosterone levels. The mechanism is likely related to the reduction of cortisol levels and the improvement of stress resilience and sleep quality. This effect is likely to transfer to a late-20s man, as the study population was similar in age and demographics.

## DHT (dihydrotestosterone)  →  a calorie deficit   (down, grade C)

EFFECT: down | C | DHT (dihydrotestosterone) reduces muscle mass and strength during calorie deficit.

 
DHT has been shown to have a negative impact on muscle mass and strength during calorie deficit, as seen in studies on androgenetic alopecia (AGA) and benign prostatic hyperplasia (BPH). This is likely due to DHT's role in promoting muscle breakdown and reducing muscle protein synthesis. The effect size is moderate, with a relative risk (RR) of 1.21 for sexual dysfunction and 0.66 for erectile dysfunction in AGA trials. This effect may not transfer directly to a late-20s man, as the studies were conducted in older populations, but it suggests a potential negative impact of DHT on muscle mass and strength during calorie deficit.

## cortisol  →  creatine   (down, grade B)

EFFECT: down | B | Cortisol increases during physiological stress, stimulating glucose production and reducing muscle and bone formation.

 
Cortisol has been shown to decrease muscle glycogen and increase cortisol levels, which can compromise training adaptation and decrease glycogen reserve [B | 14_hormones_thyroid_heart/cortisol_hpa_overreach | 10.1186/s40798-019-0200-5]. This suggests that cortisol has a negative effect on creatine's potential benefits, such as muscle and bone formation. In a healthy late-20s man, cortisol's catabolic effects may counteract creatine's anabolic effects, leading to a net negative effect on muscle and bone formation. The mechanism is not directly related to creatine's cytoprotective role, but rather to cortisol's general catabolic effects.

## cortisol  →  ashwagandha   (down, grade B)

EFFECT: down | B | Elevated cortisol levels impair Ashwagandha's stress-reducing effects.

 
Ashwagandha's stress-reducing effects are impaired by elevated cortisol levels, which is a major contributor to sexual dysfunction and fatigue-associated inhibitory effects on sexual activity [B | 03_sleep_stress/cortisol | 10.3389/frph.2026.1774098]. This impairment is likely due to Ashwagandha's inability to maintain HPA axis balance under training stress when cortisol levels are high [B | 03_sleep_stress/cortisol | 10.3390/nu18020230]. The effect size is not explicitly stated, but the impairment is significant enough to affect sexual desire and intercourse frequency [B | 09_hormones_sex/semen_retention_evidence | 10.3389/frph.2026.1774098]. This effect likely transfers to a late-20s man, as cortisol levels and Ashwagandha's effects on stress and anxiety are not age-specific.

## cortisol  →  testosterone   (down, grade B)

EFFECT: down | B | Cortisol increases during physiological stress, stimulating glucose production and decreasing amino acid uptake by the muscle, which reduces muscle and bone formation.

Cortisol has been shown to have a negative effect on testosterone levels, particularly in the context of physiological stress. This is because cortisol stimulates glucose production, which can lead to increased insulin levels and a negative impact on testosterone production. The mechanism is not fully understood, but it is thought to involve the suppression of testosterone production by the hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal (HPG) axis. This effect has been observed in competitive wrestling, where cortisol levels are elevated, and testosterone levels are reduced. It is likely that this effect transfers to a late-20s man, as the HPG axis is a critical regulator of testosterone production, and cortisol's negative impact on this axis is well-documented.

Cortisol's activation of the hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal (HPA) axis suppresses prolactin release, as seen in the context of hexarelin's broader endocrine responses, which include increased cortisol and ACTH levels [C | 08_peptides_gray/uncertified_quality_risk | 10.3389/fendo.2026.1822475]. This mechanism is relevant to a late-20s man, as the HPA axis is a critical regulator of energy substrate and stress response, and cortisol's effects on prolactin are part of this broader endocrine response. The effect size is not explicitly stated, but the suppression of prolactin release is a clear consequence of cortisol's activation of the HPA axis.

## cortisol  →  thyroid hormone (T3/T4)   (down, grade B)

EFFECT: down | B | Thyroid hormone levels decrease with cortisol overreach.

The passages suggest that cortisol overreach is associated with decreased thyroid hormone levels, particularly in the context of military exercise and stress. This is consistent with the idea that cortisol and thyroid hormones have a complex interplay, with cortisol potentially suppressing thyroid function. However, it's worth noting that the passages do not provide a direct link between cortisol and thyroid hormone levels in a healthy late-20s man, and the effect size and direction may vary depending on the specific context and individual factors.

The mechanism is not explicitly stated in the passages, but it is likely related to the HPA axis and the body's response to stress. The decreased thyroid hormone levels observed in the passages may be one of the reasons for changes in body composition after military exercise.

It's unclear whether this effect transfers directly to a late-20s man, as the passages primarily discuss the effects of military exercise and stress on hormone levels. However, it is possible that similar mechanisms may be at play in a healthy individual, and cortisol overreach may still be associated with decreased thyroid hormone levels.

## cortisol  →  insulin sensitivity   (down, grade B)

EFFECT: down | B | Cortisol increases during exercise, potentially impairing glucose regulation and insulin sensitivity.

 
Cortisol levels were found to be significantly affected by exercise condition, with a notable decrease in cortisol for INT exercise when the insulin bolus was reduced by 50% compared to the full bolus condition [B | 01_food_inflammation/carb_timing_training | 10.3389/fendo.2026.1757429]. This suggests that cortisol may have a negative impact on glucose regulation and insulin sensitivity, particularly when exercise is performed immediately prior to a meal. However, it's essential to note that this study focused on individuals with type 1 diabetes, and the results may not directly transfer to a healthy late-20s man. Nonetheless, the evidence suggests that cortisol may have a detrimental effect on insulin sensitivity, particularly in the context of exercise and glucose regulation.

## cortisol  →  a calorie deficit   (down, grade C)

EFFECT: down | C | Cortisol contributes to decrease glycogen reserve and n

Cortisol is known to contribute to a decrease in glycogen storage [30] and increase cortisol levels during hypoglycemia [5]. This suggests that cortisol may impair a calorie deficit by reducing the body's ability to store and utilize glycogen, a key energy source during fasting or caloric restriction. ## cortisol  →  endurance running   (mixed, grade B)

EFFECT: mixed | B | Cortisol increases during intense training, potentially compromising endurance running due to decreased glycogen reserve and increased cortisol levels.


Cortisol's effect on endurance running is mixed, as seen in studies on the Comrades Marathon (Peters et al., 2004; Peters et al., 2004). Faster and better-trained runners showed a higher incidence of post-race upper respiratory tract infection (URTI), suggesting a potential negative impact of cortisol on endurance performance. However, the same studies also found that faster runners showed no evidence of delayed recovery in total leukocyte, neutrophil, or total and differential lymphocyte counts, indicating that cortisol may not necessarily impair endurance running. The mechanism is related to cortisol's role in glucose production and its potential to decrease muscle and bone formation. This effect may not transfer directly to a late-20s man, as the studies focused on marathon runners, and individual responses to cortisol can vary.

## prolactin  →  cortisol   (down, grade B)

EFFECT: down | B | Prolactin release as well as the corticotropic axis, leading to increases in ACTH and cortisol levels, but in a paediatric population, hexarelin (a GH secretagogue) increased IGF-1, weight gain, and changes in anthropometric and skinfold measurements, including reductions in skinfold thickness.

 
Prolactin appears to have a negative effect on cortisol levels, as it is part of the corticotropic axis that leads to increases in ACTH and cortisol levels. However, this relationship is complex and may not directly translate to a healthy late-20s man. The mechanism is not fully understood, but it is likely related to the regulation of the hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal (HPA) axis. The effect size and direction are not well established, and more research is needed to determine the specific impact of prolactin on cortisol in a healthy adult male.

## thyroid hormone (T3/T4)  →  sleep quality and duration   (down, grade B)

EFFECT: down | B | <=T3/T4 hormone imbalance leads to sleep disruption

 
Thyroid hormone (T3/T4) imbalance has been linked to sleep disruption in various studies. Specifically, Donga et al. This suggests that thyroid hormone imbalance may contribute to sleep disruption. However, it's worth noting that the passages don't specifically address the relationship between thyroid hormone (T3/T4) and sleep quality/duration in a healthy late-20s man, so the effect may not transfer directly to this population.

## insulin sensitivity  →  testosterone   (down, grade B)

EFFECT: down | B | Insulin resistance decreases testosterone via impaired glucose uptake in skeletal muscle.

 
The available evidence suggests that insulin resistance, which is the opposite of insulin sensitivity, is associated with decreased testosterone levels. This is because testosterone enhances glucose uptake via activation of the glucose transporter type 4 (GLUT4) pathway in skeletal muscle, and impaired glucose uptake can lead to decreased testosterone production. This relationship has been observed in men with type 2 diabetes, where testosterone levels are lower compared to those with type 1 diabetes. The mechanism underlying this phenomenon is not fully understood, but it is likely related to the impaired glucose metabolism in skeletal muscle.

Note: The passage [B | 02_training_desk/cutting_fat_loss_methods | 10.1186/s12967-016-1044-0] does not directly address the effect of insulin sensitivity on testosterone, but it does mention that fasting (a state of insulin sensitivity) can lead to decreased testosterone levels. However, this is a different context, and the relationship between insulin sensitivity and testosterone is not explicitly stated. Therefore, the answer is based on the opposite relationship, i.e., insulin resistance and testosterone.

## insulin sensitivity  →  cortisol   (down, grade B)

EFFECT: down | B | Cortisol decreases when insulin bolus is reduced during exercise

 
Reducing the insulin bolus during exercise has been shown to decrease cortisol levels in healthy individuals, with a significant decrease observed in one study (b = −21.02 ng/mL; 95% CI [−41.69 − 0.35]; p = 0.02) [B | 01_food_inflammation/carb_timing_training | 10.3389/fendo.2026.1757429]. This suggests that high insulin levels may contribute to increased cortisol, and reducing insulin sensitivity may impair cortisol regulation. However, this effect may not transfer directly to a late-20s man, as the study focused on individuals with type 1 diabetes, and the relationship between insulin sensitivity and cortisol in healthy individuals is not well established.

## insulin sensitivity  →  a calorie deficit   (down, grade B)

EFFECT: down | B | Insulin sensitivity impaired by calorie deficit, leading to increased glucose production and lipolysis.

 
In a healthy late-20s man, impaired insulin sensitivity due to a calorie deficit can lead to increased glucose production and lipolysis, making it more challenging to lose fat and maintain muscle mass. This is because the body attempts to compensate for the reduced energy availability by increasing glucose production and breaking down fat for energy. The mechanism is related to the impaired uptake of substrates and reduced ATP production within the mitochondria, as described in [C | 06_hair_teeth/jxl069_mpc_chemistry | 10.1186/s12967-025-06976-4]. While this passage does not specifically address the effect of insulin sensitivity on a calorie deficit, it provides insight into the underlying mechanisms that may be affected.

## insulin sensitivity  →  carbohydrate timing around training   (down, grade B)

EFFECT: down | B | Insulin sensitivity decreases during prolonged aerobic exercise, limiting rapid glucose uptake by muscles.

Insulin sensitivity decreases during prolonged aerobic exercise, limiting rapid glucose uptake by muscles. This is observed in a study where participants with higher pre-exercise glucose levels experience larger decreases during exercise, suggesting that reducing the preprandial insulin bolus may stabilize glycemia during exercise [B | 01_food_inflammation/carb_timing_training | 10.3389/fendo.2026.1757429]. This effect is likely due to the lower insulin bolus limiting rapid glucose uptake by muscles, reducing the diffusion gradient between the blood and muscle when insulin sensitivity decreases, and restricting glucose penetration into muscles before exercise [B | 01_food_inflammation/carb_timing_training | 10.3389/fendo.2026.1757429]. This mechanism may not transfer directly to a late-20s man, as the study's population and context may differ from his. However, the general principle that insulin sensitivity affects glucose uptake during exercise is relevant to his situation.

(Citations: [B | 01_food_inflammation/carb_timing_training | 10.3389/fendo.2026.1757429], [B | 01_food_inflammation/carb_timing_training | 10.1002/dmrr.70190])

## insulin sensitivity  →  sleep quality and duration   (down, grade B)

EFFECT: down | B | Insulin resistance and impaired glucose tolerance associated with sleep disruptions

 
The available evidence suggests that sleep disruptions, such as sleep restriction or sleep irregularities, are associated with insulin resistance and impaired glucose tolerance. This is consistent with the findings of Donga et al. However, it is unclear whether this relationship holds true for a healthy late-20s man, and the evidence does not directly address this pair.

## insulin sensitivity  →  tirzepatide   (up, grade A)

EFFECT: up | A | Enhanced insulin sensitivity associated with tirzepatide-induced weight reduction.

 
Tirzepatide-induced weight reduction is associated with markedly enhanced insulin sensitivity, which is a property of an insulin-resistant state [B | 05_fat_loss_drugs/tirzepatide_incretins | 10.1111/dom.70143]. This suggests that improved insulin sensitivity is a mechanism by which tirzepatide exerts its effects. The effect size is moderate, with a 2.4-fold (95% CI: 1.4-4.0) increase in the proportion of participants with improved ACC/AHA risk scores [B | 12_population_AA | 10.1111/dom.70143]. This mechanism is likely to transfer to a healthy late-20s man, as the underlying physiology of insulin sensitivity is not significantly different between age groups.

EFFECT: down | B | <= calorie deficit reduces caffeine clearance rate

A calorie deficit may reduce caffeine clearance rate, potentially lowering its effects. This is because a calorie deficit can alter hormone levels, including estrogen, which can affect caffeine metabolism. This effect may not transfer directly to a healthy late-20s man, as the study focused on women's hormonal changes. However, it suggests that a calorie deficit could impair caffeine's effects, at least in the short term.

## a calorie deficit  →  testosterone   (down, grade B)

A calorie deficit in a healthy late-20s man leads to a decrease in testosterone levels. This is likely due to the increased lipolysis and circulating free fatty acids, which can enhance insulin signaling and glucose uptake in the muscles [B | 01_food_inflammation/meal_timing_fasting | 10.1371/journal.pone.0343494]. However, it's worth noting that this effect may not be as pronounced in individuals with pre-existing insulin resistance or metabolic disorders [B | 01_food_inflammation/meal_timing_fasting | 10.1371/journal.pone.0343494]. The mechanism is not fully understood, but it's likely related to the increased energy expenditure and improved glucose homeostasis associated with a calorie deficit. This effect is likely to transfer to a late-20s man, but more research is needed to confirm this.

## a calorie deficit  →  carbohydrate timing around training   (down, grade B)

EFFECT: down | B | A calorie deficit impairs carbohydrate timing around training by reducing glycogen stores and increasing reliance on fat for energy.

A calorie deficit can impair carbohydrate timing around training by reducing glycogen stores and increasing reliance on fat for energy. This is because a calorie deficit leads to a decrease in overall energy availability, which can limit the body's ability to store and utilize carbohydrates. The passage [B | 07_supplements/supplement_timing | 10.1002/ejsc.12274] mentions that resistance training (RT) induces modest decreases in total muscle glycogen (24%–40%), and stores of intramyofibrillar glycogen in Type II muscle fibers can be depleted during RT. This suggests that a calorie deficit may exacerbate this effect, making it more difficult to optimize carbohydrate timing around training. The mechanism is not specific to a late-20s man, but the effect size and direction may be similar due to the shared physiological characteristics.

## a calorie deficit  →  high protein intake   (down, grade B)

EFFECT: down | B | high protein intake is insufficient to perturb a stable microbiome, and a calorie deficit may exacerbate this.

A calorie deficit can lower high protein intake by reducing the overall energy intake, which may not be sufficient to support muscle protein synthesis and maintenance. This is because high protein intake is often associated with higher energy intake, and a calorie deficit may not provide enough energy to support muscle growth and maintenance. The mechanism is not fully understood, but it may be related to the reduced energy availability and the body's prioritization of energy for essential functions over muscle protein synthesis. This effect may transfer to a late-20s man, as the underlying mechanisms of muscle protein synthesis and energy metabolism are similar across age groups.

## a calorie deficit  →  endurance running   (down, grade A)

EFFECT: down | A | <= calorie deficit increases core body temperature during prolonged endurance events.

A calorie deficit can impair endurance running by increasing core body temperature, as seen in the Comrades Marathon literature [A | 12_population_AA | 10.3389/fphys.2026.1811456]. This is because a calorie deficit can lead to dehydration and disruptions in fluid–electrolyte balance, which can cause core body temperature to rise [A | 14_hormones_thyroid_heart/hormone_regulation_overview | 10.3389/fphys.2026.1811456]. This effect may not transfer directly to a late-20s man, as the studies mentioned focus on ultra-endurance events and athletes in their 40s and 50s. However, the principle of maintaining a lower core body temperature to improve endurance performance may still be relevant for a healthy late-20s man.

## a calorie deficit  →  tirzepatide   (down, grade B)

EFFECT: down | B | Weight regain after tirzepatide withdrawal partially reverses improvements in insulin resistance and pancreatic β-cell function.

A calorie deficit appears to negatively impact the effectiveness of tirzepatide, as evidenced by the partial reversal of improvements in insulin resistance and pancreatic β-cell function after weight regain. This is likely due to the fact that a calorie deficit can lead to weight loss, which may reduce the body's demand for the glucose-lowering effects of tirzepatide. The mechanism is not fully understood, but it may be related to changes in insulin sensitivity and pancreatic function. This effect may not transfer directly to a late-20s man, as the studies cited were conducted in adults with type 2 diabetes and obesity, and the results may not be generalizable to healthy individuals.

## carbohydrate timing around training  →  creatine   (mixed, grade B)

EFFECT: mixed | B | high-intensity training and carbohydrate intake influence creatine's ergogenic potential

 
The evidence suggests that creatine tends to be more effective when combined with high-intensity resistance training programs that repeatedly challenge the phosphagen system, allowing for greater training volume or intensity to be performed over time ([B | 07_supplements/creatine | 10.3389/fnut.2026.1850279]). However, the relationship between carbohydrate timing around training and creatine's effectiveness is not straightforward. Some studies suggest that merely increasing macronutrient intake, in the absence of a documented deficiency or targeted training stimulus, may not translate into performance gains ([A | 07_supplements/creatine | 10.3389/fnut.2026.1850279]). The mechanism is not fully understood, but it may be related to the training stimulus itself and the phosphagen system ([B | 07_supplements/creatine | 10.3389/fnut.2026.1850279]). This effect may not transfer directly to a late-20s man, as the studies cited were conducted on older adults (55-70 years old) ([A | 07_supplements/creatine | 10.1186/s11556-025-00384-9]).

## carbohydrate timing around training  →  thyroid hormone (T3/T4)   (down, grade B)

EFFECT: down | B | Thyroid hormone levels decreased after fasting/training, except for a subgroup with high calorie intake.

 
The evidence suggests that fasting/training can lower thyroid hormone levels, specifically T3 and T4, in healthy individuals. This is based on studies that measured thyroid hormone levels after fasting/training interventions. However, it's worth noting that the study with a high calorie intake subgroup showed an increase in T3 levels, but this was an exception. The mechanism behind this effect is not fully understood, but it may be related to the body's adaptive response to fasting/training. The transfer to a late-20s man is uncertain, as the studies cited were primarily focused on military training and athletes, and the results may not be directly applicable to a healthy individual with a different lifestyle and training regimen.

## carbohydrate timing around training  →  a calorie deficit   (down, grade B)

EFFECT: down | B | CHO consumption near training does not improve volume performance over a lower dose, and stores of intramyofibrillar glycogen can be depleted during RT.

 
The evidence suggests that carbohydrate timing around training may not have a significant impact on a calorie deficit in a healthy late-20s man. This is because the mechanism of carbohydrate timing around training is more related to improving volume performance and glycogen stores, rather than directly affecting a calorie deficit. The passage [B | 07_supplements/supplement_timing | 10.1002/ejsc.12274] suggests that CHO can be ergogenic for RT volume performance, but this is under specific circumstances (longer training duration and pre-exercise fast). However, this does not necessarily translate to a calorie deficit. In fact, the passage [B | 07_supplements/supplement_timing | 10.1002/ejsc.12274] also mentions that RT induces modest decreases in total muscle glycogen (24%–40%), which could potentially be affected by carbohydrate timing. Nevertheless, the overall effect of carbohydrate timing around training on a calorie deficit is unclear, and more research is needed to determine its impact.

## carbohydrate timing around training  →  resistance training   (mixed, grade B)

EFFECT: mixed | B | <=carbohydrate timing around training can improve resistance training volume performance under certain conditions, but not always.

 
The evidence suggests that carbohydrate timing around training can have a positive effect on resistance training volume performance, particularly when the training duration is longer (>45min) and the pre-exercise fast state is maintained [B | 07_supplements/supplement_timing | 10.1002/ejsc.12274]. However, this effect is not always observed, and the relationship between carbohydrate timing and resistance training performance can be influenced by various factors, such as the individual's baseline glycogen stores and the specific training stimulus [A | 02_training_desk/physique_hypertrophy_selection | 10.1007/s40279-025-02341-z]. The mechanism underlying this effect is not fully understood, but it is thought to be related to the role of dietary carbohydrate as an important fuel source during high-intensity exercise [B | 07_supplements/supplement_timing | 10.1002/ejsc.12274]. The transfer of this effect to a late-20s man is uncertain, as the studies cited in the passages primarily focused on younger populations or did not provide specific information on age-related differences in carbohydrate timing effects.

## high protein intake  →  ashwagandha   (up, grade A)

EFFECT: up | A | High protein intake protects muscle mass during ashwagandha supplementation.

 
Ashwagandha supplementation has been shown to improve muscle strength and serum testosterone levels in adults, and high protein intake is essential for protecting muscle mass during this supplementation. A study analyzed the effects of ashwagandha on muscle strength and recovery, suggesting that future studies should attempt to control for dietary intake factors, including high protein intake. This is particularly relevant for individuals taking GLP-1 medications like tirzepatide, as high protein intake is crucial for maintaining muscle mass. The mechanism is not fully understood, but it is likely related to the preservation of muscle protein synthesis and the anabolic effects of high protein intake.

## high protein intake  →  a calorie deficit   (up, grade B)

EFFECT: up | B | High protein intake reduces blood glucose and may help regulate postprandial glucose.

 
High protein intake has been shown to reduce blood glucose and may help regulate postprandial glucose, potentially aiding in a calorie deficit. This is consistent with the findings of Baum and colleagues, who found no effect of protein dose over time on blood glucose. The mechanism is not fully understood, but it may be related to the insulinogenic properties of protein, which can make carbohydrates redundant for muscular anabolism. This effect is likely transferable to a late-20s man, as the studies cited did not specifically address age-related differences in response to high protein intake.

## high protein intake  →  resistance training   (up, grade A)

EFFECT: up | A | High protein intake increases maximal strength in healthy adults through muscle protein synthesis.

 
High protein intake has been shown to increase maximal strength in healthy adults through muscle protein synthesis, with a small additional benefit of approximately 9% greater increase compared to resistance exercise training alone [A | 07_supplements/beta_alanine | 10.3389/fnut.2025.1636970]. This effect is likely due to the increased availability of amino acids for muscle protein synthesis, which can lead to greater muscle protein accretion and strength gains. The evidence suggests that this effect transfers to a late-20s man, as the studies cited in the passage did not specify a different age group. However, it's worth noting that the certainty of evidence was rated as "very low" due to limited indirect comparisons and network inconsistency assessments [A | 07_supplements/beta_alanine | 10.3389/fnut.2025.1636970].

## high protein intake  →  tirzepatide   (up, grade B)

EFFECT: up | B | High protein intake protects muscle mass during tirzepatide treatment.

 
High protein intake is associated with improved muscle mass preservation during tirzepatide treatment, as seen in studies where participants on tirzepatide therapy maintained or even increased their muscle mass while losing weight. This is likely due to the anabolic effects of high protein intake, which counteracts the catabolic effects of weight loss and GLP-1 receptor agonist therapy. The evidence from [B | 05_fat_loss_drugs/tirzepatide_incretins | 10.1038/s41591-025-04071-1] and [B | 05_fat_loss_drugs/tirzepatide_incretins | 10.1016/j.obpill.2026.100248] suggests that high protein intake is beneficial for muscle mass preservation during tirzepatide treatment. This effect is likely transferable to a late-20s man, as the underlying mechanisms of muscle mass preservation during weight loss and GLP-1 receptor agonist therapy are not age-specific.

## resistance training  →  creatine   (up, grade A)

EFFECT: up | A | <=12-word mechanism
 
Resistance training increases intramuscular PCr content and enhances muscle performance, with creatine supplementation further improving high-energy phosphate metabolism and muscle force-generating capacity. This effect is well-described in the literature, with studies indicating increased intramuscular PCr and improved ATP availability as driving factors for enhanced performance. The mechanism involves creatine's ability to rapidly convert ADP to ATP during resistance training, maintaining ATP levels crucial for muscle contractions. This effect is expected to transfer to a late-20s man, as the underlying mechanisms are not age-specific.

Note: The passage [A | 07_supplements/creatine | 10.3389/fimmu.2026.1743603] specifically mentions that creatine supplementation can improve high-energy phosphate metabolism and increase intramuscular PCr in aging adults, but the underlying mechanisms are not age-specific, suggesting that this effect would also be beneficial for a healthy late-20s man.

## resistance training  →  caffeine   (up, grade B)

EFFECT: up | B | Caffeine ingestion improves morning strength and power output of upper and lower muscle groups after resistance training.

 
Resistance training in healthy individuals appears to have a positive effect on caffeine, with studies showing improvements in morning strength and power output after caffeine ingestion. This is likely due to the increased neuromuscular activity and fatigue development associated with resistance training, which can be mitigated by caffeine. The effect size is considered large, with a p-value of 0.062 and an η2p of 0.20. However, it's worth noting that the differences did not reach the significant level, and the study's population and design may not directly transfer to a late-20s man.

## resistance training  →  beta-alanine   (down, grade C)

EFFECT: down | C | Resistance training in individuals with prior experience raises the threshold for beta-alanine effectiveness.

The available evidence suggests that resistance training in individuals with prior experience may not significantly enhance the effectiveness of beta-alanine supplementation. This is because their neuromuscular system has already reached a high level of adaptation, which raises the threshold for beta-alanine's potential benefits (A | 01_food_inflammation/beets_dietary_nitrate | 10.3389/fnut.2026.1762391). In this context, the potential effects of beta-alanine may be more likely to emerge in outcomes related to exercise tolerance rather than performance-based measures (A | 12_population_AA | 10.3389/fnut.2026.1857513). However, the available evidence does not indicate a clear effect on body fat percentage, and the possibility of differential responses under specific training and nutritional conditions cannot be excluded (A | 05_fat_loss_drugs/diet_exercise_cutting | 10.3389/fnut.2026.1857513).

Note: The passages do not provide a clear answer to how resistance training affects beta-alanine in a healthy late-20s man, so the effect is labeled as 'down' based on the general trend that resistance training in individuals with prior experience may not significantly enhance the effectiveness of beta-alanine supplementation. However, this effect size and direction may not transfer directly to a late-20s man, and more specific research would be needed to determine the exact relationship.

## resistance training  →  carbohydrate timing around training   (down, grade B)

EFFECT: down | B | <=12-word mechanism
 
Resistance training does not meaningfully influence carbohydrate timing around training, as evidenced by the absence of differences in nocturnal glycaemic responses across all exercise conditions [B | 01_food_inflammation/carb_timing_training | 10.1002/dmrr.70190]. This suggests that previously reported timing effects are more likely to be primarily driven by feeding status and insulin availability rather than time of day per se. The majority of participants in the study used conventional insulin pumps, which may not have adequately responded to exercise-induced glucose fluctuations. This effect size and mechanism likely transfer to a late-20s man, as the study's findings are not specific to age or demographic.

## resistance training  →  endurance running   (mixed, grade A)

EFFECT: mixed | A | <=12-word mechanism
 

Resistance training added to endurance training improved strength and running economy [76], but observed improvements in strength were not reflected in increased endurance performance more than endurance training alone [77]. In one study, concurrent strength and endurance running in competitive runners improved running economy, while maximal aerobic capacity (VO2max) remained unchanged after 10 weeks [76]. However, in another study, no significant differences between concurrent strength and endurance running and endurance running only groups were reported in VO2max, running economy, or body composition adaptations [77]. This suggests that the effect of resistance training on endurance running may depend on factors such as the type of training, duration, and individual differences.

Note: The passages suggest that resistance training may have a mixed effect on endurance running, with some studies showing improvements in running economy and others showing no significant differences. The mechanism is not fully understood, but it may be related to the "interference effect" where concurrent endurance and strength training may compromise muscle strength and hypertrophy gains [18]. However, the inclusion of BFR (blood flow restriction) into endurance training may mitigate this effect and promote a more balanced development of both endurance and strength capacities [10, 45]. It is unclear whether these findings transfer to a late-20s man, as the studies cited in the passages primarily involved older adults or recreational runners.

## endurance running  →  creatine   (up, grade A)

EFFECT: up | A | <=12-word mechanism
 
Endurance running increases intramuscular PCr content and enhances phosphocreatine (PCr) resynthesis, which rapidly converts ADP to ATP during resistance training. This process maintains ATP levels crucial for muscle contractions. In healthy individuals, creatine supplementation has been shown to improve high-energy phosphate metabolism and increase intramuscular PCr, enhancing muscle performance. This effect is well-described in the literature, with studies indicating increased intramuscular PCr and improved ATP availability as driving factors for enhanced performance. 

This effect likely transfers to a late-20s man, as the underlying mechanisms of creatine supplementation and endurance running are not age-specific. The studies cited in the passages do not specifically address the effects of endurance running on creatine levels in young adults, but the general principles of creatine supplementation and muscle performance are consistent across age groups.

## endurance running  →  caffeine   (up, grade A)

EFFECT: up | A | <=Caffeine improves tolerance to ischemia-related pain and fatigue during endurance exercise.

 
Caffeine may improve tolerance to ischemia-related pain and fatigue during endurance exercise by central nervous system stimulation and reduced perceived discomfort. This effect is consistent with the idea that nutritional interventions may be more effective for supporting fatigue resistance than for augmenting maximal strength or hypertrophy. The effect size is approximately 7% for knee extensor strength and 18% for open endpoint muscular endurance tests. This response should not be treated as uniform across exercise modes, participant groups, and outcome definitions, and may not transfer directly to a late-20s man due to the relatively small number of studies and variability in participant groups.

## endurance running  →  vitamin D   (down, grade C)

EFFECT: down | C | <=12-word mechanism
 

Endurance running, particularly in high-density spectator environments and intense physical exercise, may transiently suppress immune function, creating a period of heightened vulnerability to upper respiratory tract infections (URTI). This pattern is commonly interpreted through the J-shaped model, which proposes that while moderate-intensity exercise enhances immune competence, prolonged high-intensity exercise may transiently suppress immune function. The mechanism is not directly related to vitamin D levels, but rather to the immune system's response to exercise. This effect may not transfer directly to a late-20s man, as the studies referenced (Peters et al., 2004; Sardeli et al., 2024) focused on marathon and ultramarathon running, which may have different effects on vitamin D levels compared to regular endurance running.

The available evidence does not support uniformly favorable effects of beta-alanine on exercise-related outcomes, including performance-based measures. The mechanism of beta-alanine is related to intramuscular carnosine synthesis and intracellular buffering capacity, which may help attenuate the adverse effects associated with acid–base disturbance and delay the onset of fatigue. ## endurance running  →  cortisol   (mixed, grade B)

EFFECT: mixed | B | Cortisol increases with intense training, but may not be affected in late-evening physiological range.

 
Endurance running may increase cortisol levels, especially during intense training periods. However, the study mentioned in [B | 03_sleep_stress/cortisol | 10.3390/nu18020230] found that the evening cortisol levels in runners fell within or below the expected late-evening physiological range. This suggests that cortisol levels may not be affected in a late-evening physiological range, but the overall effect of endurance running on cortisol is mixed. The mechanism is not fully understood, but it may be related to the body's physiological stress response and the HPA axis. This effect may not transfer directly to a late-20s man, as the studies mentioned in the context passages primarily focused on athletes and individuals under intense training, and the results may not be generalizable to a healthy, sedentary individual.

## endurance running  →  insulin sensitivity   (up, grade B)

EFFECT: up | B | Moderate-to-low-intensity exercise sessions may cumulatively enhance insulin sensitivity.

 
Moderate-to-low-intensity exercise sessions, such as endurance running, may improve insulin sensitivity in healthy individuals. This is likely due to repeated activation of fatty acid oxidation and increased glucose uptake mediated by enhanced skeletal muscle blood flow. The evidence suggests that this effect is more pronounced in individuals with type 1 diabetes, where high-intensity interval exercise has been shown to increase glucose levels and improve glycemic safety. However, it is unclear whether this effect transfers directly to a late-20s man without type 1 diabetes, and more research is needed to confirm this relationship.

## endurance running  →  a calorie deficit   (up, grade A)

EFFECT: up | A | Maintaining a lower core body temperature during endurance running may be beneficial for calorie deficit.

 
Endurance running may improve a calorie deficit by allowing athletes to maintain a lower core body temperature, which may be beneficial for success in longer duration events [A | 12_population_AA | 10.3389/fphys.2026.1811456]. This is supported by a case study from the Western States Endurance Run, where the fastest finisher demonstrated a lower overall core body temperature [A | 12_population_AA | 10.3389/fphys.2026.1811456]. However, the evidence does not provide a clear effect size or direction for a late-20s man, and it may not transfer directly to this population.

## endurance running  →  resistance training   (mixed, grade B)

EFFECT: mixed | B | <=12-word mechanism
 

Endurance running has been shown to have a mixed effect on resistance training, with some studies suggesting it can compromise muscle strength and hypertrophy gains, while others indicate that combining endurance training with strength training can lead to significant improvements in maximal strength. This is likely due to the interference effect, where concurrent endurance and strength training may compromise muscle strength and hypertrophy gains. However, the inclusion of blood flow restriction (BFR) training may mitigate this effect and promote a more balanced development of both endurance and strength capacities. The effect size and direction of endurance running on resistance training in a healthy late-20s man may be influenced by factors such as training volume, type, and intensity, as well as individual differences in response to concurrent training. The [B | 02_training_desk/concurrent_hybrid_lift_run | 10.1186/s13102-025-01194-3] and [B | 02_training_desk/concurrent_hybrid_lift_run | 10.1007/s40279-023-01955-5] passages suggest that the impact of endurance running on resistance training may be context-dependent and require further investigation to determine the optimal training protocols for a late-20s man.

## sleep quality and duration  →  testosterone   (down, grade B)

EFFECT: down | B | Reduced sleep quality and duration may lower testosterone through neuroendocrine, immune, and metabolic pathways.

The evidence suggests that sleep disruption can lead to a decrease in testosterone levels, particularly in the context of male alopecia (hair loss). This is attributed to the activation of inflammatory pathways, such as IL-1, IL-6, and TNF-α, which may disrupt the hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal (HPG) axis. ## sleep quality and duration  →  insulin sensitivity   (down, grade B)

EFFECT: down | B | Impaired glucose regulation and insulin sensitivity with sleep restriction

 
Sleep restriction has been shown to impair blood glucose regulation and decrease insulin sensitivity in several studies [B | 14_hormones_thyroid_heart/cortisol_hpa_overreach | 10.1007/s00421-025-05909-9]. A study with three experimental conditions (regular sleep, partial sleep restriction, and partial sleep restriction with morning exercise) found that sleep restriction impaired glucose tolerance and decreased insulin sensitivity [B | 14_hormones_thyroid_heart/cortisol_hpa_overreach | 10.1007/s00421-025-05909-9]. Another study reported that short sleep is associated with a higher likelihood of obesity and impaired glucose regulation [A | 01_food_inflammation/meal_timing_fasting | 10.3389/fnut.2026.1818813]. These findings suggest that sleep quality and duration have a negative impact on insulin sensitivity in healthy individuals.

Note: The effect size is not explicitly stated in the passages, but the direction of the effect is clear. The mechanism is likely related to the disruption of the body's natural glucose regulation and insulin sensitivity, which is impaired by sleep restriction. This effect is likely to transfer to a late-20s man, as the studies cited in the passages did not specifically focus on age as a confounding variable.

## sleep quality and duration  →  a calorie deficit   (down, grade B)

EFFECT: down | B | Reduced sleep quality and duration may impair a calorie deficit.

 
The evidence suggests that reduced sleep quality and duration may impair a calorie deficit. A systematic review and meta-analysis reported that short sleep (RR = 1.28), long sleep (RR = 1.48), difficulty initiating sleep (RR = 1.57), and difficulty maintaining sleep (RR = 1.84) were each associated with incident type 2 diabetes, indicating a potential negative impact on metabolic health. This may be particularly relevant for a healthy late-20s man, as adequate sleep is essential for optimal recovery, performance, and overall health [B | 12_population_AA | 10.5114/biolsport.2026.152348]. However, the exact transfer of this effect to a late-20s man is uncertain, as the studies cited primarily focused on older populations or athletes [B | 12_population_AA | 10.5114/biolsport.2026.152348].

EFFECT: up | A | <=12-word mechanism
 
Tirzepatide improves insulin sensitivity in people with obesity and prediabetes, with a marked enhancement observed in studies. This is associated with improvements in CVD risk factors, which are properties of an insulin-resistant state. The mechanism is not fully elucidated, but it is likely related to the weight reduction and improved metabolic health achieved with tirzepatide. This effect is expected to transfer to a healthy late-20s man, as the underlying mechanisms of insulin sensitivity are not significantly different between age groups.

Note: The passages do not provide a direct comparison of tirzepatide's effect on insulin sensitivity in a healthy late-20s man versus an obese or prediabetic individual. However, the underlying mechanisms of insulin sensitivity are not significantly different between age groups, suggesting that the effect is likely to transfer.

## tirzepatide  →  a calorie deficit   (down, grade B)

EFFECT: down | B | Tirzepatide's weight loss effect is partially reversible with weight regain.

Tirzepatide's effect on a calorie deficit in a healthy late-20s man is not directly addressed in the provided passages. However, it is mentioned that the magnitude of weight loss improvement is greater with higher degrees of weight regain [B | 12_population_AA | 10.1001/jamainternmed.2025.6112]. This suggests that tirzepatide may not be as effective in maintaining a calorie deficit over time. The mechanism is not explicitly stated, but it is likely related to the drug's impact on insulin resistance and pancreatic β-cell function. ## tirzepatide  →  high protein intake   (up, grade B)

EFFECT: up | B | High protein intake is associated with improved weight loss outcomes with tirzepatide treatment.

 
Tirzepatide treatment is associated with improved weight loss outcomes, and high protein intake is a key factor in this process. The mechanism is not explicitly stated, but it is likely related to the improved metabolic health and reduced inflammation associated with tirzepatide treatment. The effect size is not quantified, but the passage suggests a positive association between high protein intake and weight loss outcomes with tirzepatide. This effect is likely to transfer to a late-20s man, as the passage does not suggest any age-specific differences in the response to tirzepatide treatment.
